Date: 08 June 2026

1. General Guidelines & Collaboration
   * Source Material: Refer strictly to the designated book(s) when creating questions.
   * File Management: Keep all content consolidated within one single Google Doc.
   * AI Tooling Restriction: Avoid using LLMs (Large Language Models) during the initial idea phase.
   * Team Dynamics: Focus on increased collaboration across the entire team.
2. Workflow Pipeline & Responsibilities
The task pipeline progresses through the following designated roles:
   * Shreya: Responsible for creating the assessment matrix.
   * Sejal: Responsible for researching/mapping how a child thinks.
   * Prajakta: Assist Arnab and Aman with simplifying questions.
   * Arnab & Aman: Responsible for the final creation of the questions.
3. Question Design & Leveling Strategy
   * Comparison Concepts:  Comparison (=, <, >) must be kept strictly at Level 1. *Merge any more levels which appear so similar.
   * Research Action Item: Investigate why comparison is chosen for the 1st level instead of other concepts.
   * Level Structure:
   * Level: Focus on 1 concept only, keeping the cognitive load light.
   * Sublevel: Present the same concept but framed in an easier, more accessible way.
   * Question Formats: Use Matching Questions instead of designing separate, individual equivalence questions.
4. Document Structure Updates
   * New Addition: Add a dedicated Summary Tab to the primary project document.

B. Web Interface & Content Management
   * Webpage Design: The main webpage interface will feature a tabbed layout. Each tab will be dedicated to displaying specific questions.
   * File Separation: Individual questions or data sets must be managed within separate .md files rather than a single monolithic file.
C. Assessment Design & Evaluation (FLN Test)
   * Test Architecture: Reviewing the foundational logic of the FLN (Foundational Literacy and Numeracy) test. If it is verified as a strictly level-based assessment, each student must receive a separate, randomized question set.
   * Quality Assurance: The evaluation rubric for reviewing and grading the question paper needs to be formally submitted to the supervisor/reviewer.
D. Upcoming Presentation Requirements
The upcoming project presentation must follow a standardized structure, limiting allocation to 1–2 slides per team member. The slide deck must cover:
   1. Problem Statement: Clearly defining the core challenge.
   2. Pipeline: Outlining the technical/operational workflow.
   3. Progress: Highlighting achievements and current status.

NIPUN Bharat link
NIPUN is competency-based, not syllabus-based. 

Like focuses more on taking regular assessment to judge a child progress more than 2 or 3 yearly assessments which focuses more on ROTE method (repeating information over and over until it is memorized mechanically, often without a deep understanding of the underlying 
 concept )

Pg-23 link


NIPUN metric to determine a child competency


Decision-Making Skills
Children should learn to:
   1. Identify a problem
   2. Gather information
   3. Explore alternatives
   4. Evaluate options
   5. Make informed decisions

Ex 
questions like:
You have ₹20 and two items cost ₹12 and ₹8. Can you buy both?
over
12 + 8 = ?
Organizing Skills
Children should develop habits such as:
      * Following routines
      * Managing belongings
      * Completing tasks systematically
      * Working cooperatively
These habits help build:
      * Self-discipline
      * Focus
      * Responsibility
Evaluation Implication
Usually assessed through teacher observation rather than written tests.
Communication Skills
Children should learn to:
      * Express ideas clearly
      * Explain their thinking
      * Listen to others
      * Participate in discussions
Ex 
Instead of only asking:
What is the answer?
Also ask:
How did you get the answer?
This reveals the child's reasoning process.
Curiosity and Inquiry
Children should:
      * Ask questions
      * Explore ideas
      * Connect new learning with prior experiences
      * Apply knowledge in real-world situations
Evaluation Implication
Assessment should encourage exploration and reasoning rather than only fixed procedures.

complete official IL numeracy LO table (pg 27-33)

IL - Involved Learners — it is the code for Developmental Goal 3 in NIPUN Bharat. 
LO = Learning Outcome — a specific, measurable statement of what a child should be able to do at a particular grade level. 
The prefix changes from IL to ILM at Class 1 — the M signals the shift from general environmental learning into formal Mathematics.

	Code

	Beginner
	Level 0

	Number Recognition 1–9
	Level 1

	Class 1
	Number Recognition 11–99
	Level 2
	Correctly identifies at least 4 out of 5 two-digit numbers
	Class 1–2
	Subtraction
	Level 3
	Solves 2-digit subtraction with borrowing
	Class 2
	Division
	Level 4
	Solves 3-digit ÷ 1-digit division with remainder

State-Wise-Data
MATHEMATICS CURRICULUM                    ANALYSIS
Classes 1 to 4 — Indian States & School Boards


FLN-Focused Deep Curriculum Research Report
Aligned with NEP 2020 | NIPUN Bharat Framework | ASER Standards
June 2026
Scope: 29 States | 4 Classes | 100+ Textbook Links Analysed




State wise Syllabus sheet : Maths_syllabus
Class 1 FLN criteria:-  FLN_Class1_Maths_Assessment_Report
Class 2 FLN criteria :- FLN_Diagnostic_Assessment_Class2
Class 3 FLN criteria :- FLN_Diagnostic_Assessment_Class3
1. Executive Summary
This report presents a comprehensive, evidence-based analysis of Mathematics curricula for Classes 1–4 across 29 Indian states and union territories. The study draws on textbooks, syllabi, and pedagogical frameworks accessed via official state board links, cross-referenced with publicly available SCERT and board publications, academic literature on FLN, and NEP 2020 guidelines.


Key findings at a glance:
      * Kerala (SCERT/KBPE) leads all states in FLN alignment, constructivist pedagogy, and activity-based learning — scoring 9/10 across all four classes.
      * Tamil Nadu (Samacheer Kalvi) demonstrates the strongest overall curriculum rigor and fastest concept progression, with the highest number range (up to 99,999) reached by Class 4.
      * Maharashtra (Balbharati) and Mizoram (SCERT) are standout performers for activity-based learning and contextual problem-solving.
      * 11 states (Arunachal Pradesh, Goa, Haryana, Himachal Pradesh, J&K, Manipur, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Tripura, Uttarakhand, Sikkim) follow the NCERT framework with little or no state adaptation, producing an 'Average' classification.
 Note: Textbook links were available only for Haryana, J&K, and Sikkim among this group; the remaining 8 states are analysed based on known board affiliations and cross-reference sources. 
      * Bihar presents the weakest FLN alignment, with evidence of rote-learning emphasis, limited activity components, and the lowest number range in Classes 1–2.
      * Most states reach four-digit numbers (up to 9,999) by Class 4, with Tamil Nadu and Kerala extending to five-digit numbers, indicating advanced number sense development.
      * Geometry coverage is universally present but varies significantly — Kerala and Tamil Nadu embed spatial reasoning from Class 1 through investigative activities, while several northern states treat it as secondary.
      * Data handling appears explicitly from Class 2 in Tamil Nadu, Kerala, and Andhra Pradesh, but is absent or minimally treated in Bihar, Chhattisgarh, and Rajasthan at primary level.


NEP 2020 & NIPUN Bharat Alignment:
NEP 2020 mandates Foundational Stage (Grades 1–2) focus on play-based, experiential learning, and Preparatory Stage (Grades 3–5) focus on conceptual building. The NIPUN Bharat mission targets Grade 3 numeracy competencies including: counting to 999, 2-digit addition/subtraction, multiplication tables up to 5, and simple measurement. Against this benchmark, roughly 60% of state curricula are on track, while 25% are ahead of schedule (Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, Mizoram) and 15% fall behind (Bihar, some UTs).
2. Methodology
2.1 Data Collection
      * 116 Mathematics textbook links covering Classes 1–4 across 29 states and union territories were reviewed. Of these, 37 NCERT ZIP links (covering Arunachal Pradesh, Goa, Himachal Pradesh, Manipur, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Tripura, Uttarakhand fully, and Class 1 links for Odisha and Uttar Pradesh) were removed from the final dataset as state-specific syllabi were unavailable. These states are retained in the analysis using cross-reference sources. 
      * Most textbooks were accessed through official SCERT and state board sources.
      * For around 9 states where textbooks were unavailable online, analysis was based on official syllabi and curriculum documents.
2.2 Analytical Framework
Each curriculum was assessed using a 14-dimension framework:
      * Number sense development trajectory
      * Arithmetic operation progression (addition, subtraction, multiplication, division)
      * Place value depth and sequencing
      * Geometry and spatial reasoning
      * Measurement concepts (length, weight, capacity, time, money)
      * Data handling and representation
      * Patterns and logical reasoning
      * Problem-solving and word problems
      * Activity-based and experiential learning
      * Mental mathematics
      * Assessment approach
      * FLN alignment (against NIPUN Bharat benchmarks)
      * Conceptual vs rote learning balance
      * Inclusivity and child-centred design


2.3 Scoring Rubric
FLN Alignment Score (1–10): Measures how closely the curriculum aligns to NIPUN Bharat competency benchmarks, NEP 2020 pedagogical principles, and FLN best practices. A score of 9–10 indicates excellent alignment; 7–8 is good; 5–6 is partial; below 5 is poor alignment.
Difficulty Score (1–10): Measures curricular load, concept density, and expected competency level relative to class. Scores above 8 indicate advanced/above national average; 6–7 is standard; below 6 is light load.
Activity Level: Very High / High / Medium / Low — based on evidence of hands-on, contextual, and investigative tasks in textbooks.
Conceptual Understanding: Constructivist / Conceptual / Traditional / Rote-heavy — based on pedagogical approach observed.
3. State-Wise Curriculum Analysis
3.1 Andhra Pradesh
Board: SCERT Andhra Pradesh | Structure: Semester-based (Classes 1–2: 2 semesters; Classes 3–4: Single book)
Curriculum Summary
AP SCERT has developed a progressive, activity-oriented curriculum. Class 1 (Semesters 1 & 2) introduces numbers up to 50, basic shapes, and foundational addition/subtraction through story-based contexts. Class 2 extends to 100 with 2-digit operations and introduces weight/capacity measurement. Class 3 introduces multiplication tables, 3-digit numbers to 1,000, fractions (half/quarter), and bar charts. Class 4 covers all four operations, large numbers up to 10,000, geometry (angles, triangles, quadrilaterals), fractions, and data handling.
Strengths
      * Strong problem-solving focus with real-life contexts from rural/village settings
      * Explicit data handling from Class 2 onwards
      * Fractions introduced early and systematically in Class 3
      * Good spiral curriculum design — concepts revisited with increasing complexity
Weaknesses
      * Limited mental mathematics activities in Classes 1–2
      * Division not explicitly covered until Class 4
      * Geometry could be strengthened in Classes 1–2
Unique Features
      * Semester structure allows deeper coverage per topic
      * Strong bilingual support (Telugu/English) with culturally contextualised word problems
FLN Readiness Assessment
NIPUN-aligned for Classes 1–3; Class 4 exceeds NIPUN benchmarks. Demonstrates good conceptual progression.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average


3.2 Kerala
Board: SCERT Kerala / KBPE | Structure: Semester-based (2 books per class)
Curriculum Summary
Kerala's KBPE curriculum stands as the gold standard of FLN-aligned, constructivist mathematics education in India. Rooted in activity-based and investigative learning, even Class 1 students engage with mathematics through games, stories, and explorations rather than drill. The textbooks ('On a Vacation', 'Wheel of Time') embed math in rich narrative contexts. Number sense is built through grouping, comparing, and estimation before formal symbolic notation. Classes 3–4 tackle multiplication and division through equal grouping activities, fractions through fair sharing, and geometry through paper folding and pattern making. Data collection projects appear in Class 3. By Class 4, students are solving multi-step problems, interpreting graphs, and working with 5-digit numbers.
Strengths
      * Best-in-class activity-based and investigative learning design
      * Constructivist pedagogy — students discover rules rather than memorise them
      * Excellent integration of real-life contexts and child-friendly narratives
      * Strong fractions and early algebra thinking from Class 3
      * Very high teacher guidance quality embedded in textbooks
      * Explicitly inclusive design — multiple representations, multiple entry points
Weaknesses
      * Requires highly trained teachers; without proper training, implementation quality suffers
      * Lighter on computational drill — students may need supplementary practice for speed
Unique Features
      * Curriculum is built around 'big ideas' — measurement, patterns, number relationships
      * Chapter titles reflect contexts, not topics — 'On a Vacation' covers measurement, 'Adding without Adding' teaches mental strategies
      * Strongest data handling component in the country at primary level
FLN Readiness Assessment
Exceeds NIPUN Bharat benchmarks at every grade level. The constructivist framework ensures deep, durable understanding — the ideal FLN model for India.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Advanced


3.3 Tamil Nadu
Board: SCERT Tamil Nadu (Samacheer Kalvi) | Structure: Term-based (3 terms per class)
Curriculum Summary
Tamil Nadu's Samacheer Kalvi curriculum is one of the most comprehensive and rigorously structured in India. The three-term structure distributes content evenly. Class 1 introduces numbers to 99, basic addition/subtraction, shapes, and simple patterns. Class 2 extends to 999 with regrouping, introduces capacity and weight, and includes pictographs. Class 3 reaches 9,999, introduces multiplication/division, fractions (halves/quarters/thirds), and bar charts. Class 4 extends to 99,999, covers all four operations with large numbers, advanced fractions, full geometry (area, perimeter, angles), and data handling including interpretation of tables and graphs. The curriculum features strong problem-solving sections and experiential activities in every chapter.
Strengths
      * Fastest concept progression among all states — reaches 5-digit numbers by Class 4
      * Comprehensive coverage: all domains present and well-developed at each class
      * Strong problem-solving emphasis across all classes
      * Term structure allows targeted reinforcement
      * Information Processing (data handling) is a dedicated topic in Class 3 and 4
Weaknesses
      * Pace may be too fast for struggling learners — high difficulty score in Classes 3–4
      * Activity component, while present, is less exploratory than Kerala's
Unique Features
      * Standalone 'Information Processing' chapter at Class 3 — unique in India at this level
      * Fractions introduced across three consecutive terms in Class 3 — deepest treatment nationally
      * Area and perimeter concepts introduced in Class 4 — ahead of NCERT trajectory
FLN Readiness Assessment
Well above NIPUN Bharat benchmarks. Suitable for students with good teacher support. Strong model for FLN excellence.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Advanced


3.4 Maharashtra
Board: MSBSHSE (Balbharati) | Structure: Single textbook per class
Curriculum Summary
Maharashtra's Balbharati textbooks are well-crafted with a balance of conceptual understanding and procedural fluency. Class 1 introduces numbers to 50 through activity-rich contexts. Class 2 covers 2-digit operations, shapes, and basic measurement. Class 3 is particularly strong — it introduces multiplication tables, division, basic fractions, perimeter, and bar graphs in a well-sequenced manner. Class 4 covers large numbers (10,000), all four operations, fractions with denominators, detailed geometry (triangles, quadrilaterals, circles), all measurement units, and data charts. The textbooks feature many 'Let's Do' activity sections and contextual word problems.
Strengths
      * Strong perimeter/area and geometry content from Class 3
      * High activity level in all four books
      * Contextual word problems rooted in Maharashtra's diverse geography and culture
      * Good teacher support features in textbooks
Weaknesses
      * Data handling coverage is lighter than AP and TN in Classes 1–2
      * Mental mathematics sections could be more structured
FLN Readiness Assessment
Strong FLN alignment — meets and slightly exceeds NIPUN benchmarks. Particularly commendable at Class 3 level.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average


3.5 Mizoram
Board: SCERT Mizoram | Structure: Semester-based | Books: Joyful Mathematics (Cl.1-2), Maths Fest (Cl.3), Math Magic IV (Cl.4)
Curriculum Summary
Mizoram's SCERT has developed a distinctly joyful, activity-centered curriculum. The 'Joyful Mathematics' series for Classes 1–2 embeds mathematics in games, nature explorations, and community contexts. Maths Fest (Class 3) emphasises collaborative, festival-themed activities for multiplication, measurement, and patterns. Math Magic IV retains high activity levels while introducing fractions and data handling. The curriculum shows strong FLN alignment with explicit emphasis on making mathematics meaningful through cultural connection.
Strengths
      * Highest activity level outside Kerala — 'Very High' in Classes 1–2
      * Strong cultural contextualisation — reflects Mizo community life
      * Accessible language and visuals, highly suitable for tribal/indigenous learners
Weaknesses
      * Some topics covered less deeply than national average (e.g., geometry in Class 1)
      * Division coverage lighter in Class 3 compared to TN/Kerala
FLN Readiness Assessment
Strong FLN alignment — unique model of culturally embedded foundational learning. Recommended for replication in Northeast India.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average


3.6 Karnataka
Board: DSERT Karnataka | Structure: Semester-based (Classes 3–4); Single book (Classes 1–2)
Karnataka's curriculum demonstrates steady progression from foundational to conceptual mathematics. Class 1 introduces shapes and numbers to 50 with activity support. The semester structure from Class 3 allows Karnataka to cover all four operations, fractions, and data handling by Class 4. The DSERT textbooks include investigative activities and problems rooted in Karnataka's geography, history, and crafts. Above Average overall; strong FLN readiness.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average | FLN Score: Class 3–4: 8/10


3.7 Telangana
Board: SCERT Telangana | Structure: Single textbook per class
Telangana closely mirrors AP's curriculum structure but with greater emphasis on problem-solving in urban contexts. The SCERT Telangana textbooks include well-designed word problems with multi-step reasoning from Class 3. Fractions appear early (Class 3), data handling is explicit, and geometry is progressive. Categorized as Above Average with strong FLN alignment.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average | FLN Score: Consistent 8/10


3.8 West Bengal
Board: WBBPE | Structure: Single textbook per class
West Bengal's primary mathematics curriculum (Amar Ganit, Patabhar series) shows a sound structural framework. The textbooks are more traditional in approach, with cleaner procedure-based explanations. However, Class 3–4 books (especially 'Amar Ganit') demonstrate good conceptual depth in number operations, geometry, and basic data handling. The curriculum lacks some of the exploratory activities found in Kerala/TN but is solidly Above Average for FLN.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Above Average (Classes 3–4) | FLN Score: 7–8/10


3.9 Punjab
Board: PSEB | Structure: Single textbook per class
Punjab's PSEB curriculum follows a structured, procedurally oriented approach. Content coverage is age-appropriate and complete. However, activity-based learning is limited compared to southern states. Mental mathematics is present but not systematically developed. The curriculum is solidly Average with moderate FLN alignment. Word problems use Punjab's agrarian and urban contexts well.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Average | FLN Score: 7/10


3.10 Gujarat
Board: GCERT | Structure: Single textbook per class
Gujarat's GCERT curriculum demonstrates above-average concept density for Classes 3–4. The textbooks include good activity sections and culturally relevant problems. Geometry and measurement are treated comprehensively from Class 3. The curriculum is classified as Average to Above Average with FLN scores of 7–8.
Overall Difficulty Classification: Average–Above Average | FLN Score: 7–8/10


3.11 NCERT-Following States
States adopting NCERT without significant adaptation (syllabus links available): Haryana, Jammu & Kashmir, Sikkim. Note: Arunachal Pradesh, Goa, Himachal Pradesh, Manipur, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Tripura, and Uttarakhand remain in the dataset but textbook links were unavailable; analysis for these states is based on known board affiliations and cross-reference sources only. 


The NCERT Math-Magic series (Classes 1–4) is a well-respected, FLN-aligned curriculum. Class 1 (13 chapters) covers shapes/space, numbers 1–50, addition, subtraction, time, measurement, data handling, patterns, and money. Class 2 (15 chapters) extends to 2-digit operations, weight, capacity, patterns, and data. Class 3 (14 chapters) introduces multiplication, division, fractions, geometry, measurement, and data. Class 4 (14 chapters) covers all operations, fractions, area, perimeter, geometry, and charts. The NCERT curriculum is solid but does not leverage regional/cultural contexts — a missed opportunity for contextual FLN.
      * Strengths: Comprehensive, child-friendly, well-illustrated, consistent national standard
      * Weaknesses: Generic contexts; no regional adaptation; activity level varies by chapter
Overall Difficulty Classification: Average | FLN Score: 7/10 across Classes 1–4


3.12 Remaining States
Assam / Odisha (SCERT)
Both states have developed state-specific curricula with moderate FLN alignment. Assam's curriculum incorporates Assamese cultural contexts effectively. Odisha shows stronger activity design than Bihar or MP, particularly in Odiya-medium books. FLN Score: 7/10. Classification: Average.
Madhya Pradesh
SCERT MP curriculum is structured but leans traditional. Limited activity components in Classes 1–2. Class 3–4 coverage improves. FLN Score: 6–7/10. Classification: Average.
Chhattisgarh / Rajasthan
Both states show adequate coverage with limited activity-based components. Data handling is minimal in Classes 1–2. Rajasthan has improved its Class 3–4 textbooks with better problem-solving sections. FLN Score: 6–7/10. Classification: Average.
Uttar Pradesh
UP's SCERT 'Gintara' series (Classes 3–4) shows notable improvement with good contextual problems and activity sections. Classes 1–2 follow NCERT. FLN Score: 6–7/10. Classification: Average, improving.
Bihar
Bihar's curriculum shows the weakest FLN alignment among the surveyed states. Limited activity components, heavy reliance on procedural drill, minimal data handling in Classes 1–2, and the narrowest number range (to 20 in Class 1). FLN Score: 6/10. Classification: Below Average.
Jharkhand
JAC curriculum partially follows NCERT with state adaptations. Coverage is adequate but less contextual than neighbouring Odisha or West Bengal. FLN Score: 6–7/10. Classification: Average.
4. Class-Wise Analysis
4.1 Class 1 — Foundational Stage Analysis
Key domains assessed: Number sense (1–50), Shapes/Space, Basic addition/subtraction, Measurement readiness, Patterns, Early data.
Most Advanced Curriculum: Kerala SCERT — activity-based number exploration, spatial reasoning, and early mathematical communication.
Most FLN-Aligned: Kerala (9/10), Mizoram (8/10), Maharashtra (8/10)
Fastest Concept Progression: Tamil Nadu — reaches numbers to 99 by end of Class 1; introduces pictographs and basic data.
Slowest Concept Progression: Bihar — numbers only to 20 by end of Class 1; limited topics covered.
Common Gap Observed: Mental mathematics and estimation — rarely explicitly addressed in any state's Class 1 curriculum.
Curriculum Gaps: Data handling is absent or incidental in 60% of states at Class 1; Division readiness activities largely missing.


4.2 Class 2 — Expanding Foundations
Key domains: Numbers to 100, 2-digit operations, Weight/Capacity, Patterns, Basic data handling, Introduction of place value.
Most Advanced: Tamil Nadu (to 999), Kerala (conceptual depth).
Most FLN-Aligned: Kerala (9/10), Tamil Nadu (8/10), Maharashtra (8/10), Mizoram (8/10)
Fastest Progression: Tamil Nadu extends to 3-digit numbers by end of Class 2 — a full class level ahead of most states.
Slowest Progression: Bihar — covers the least conceptual ground; stays below 100 for most of Class 2.
Common Gap: Capacity measurement is neglected in 40% of states at Class 2; Fractions not introduced in any state except Tamil Nadu at this level.


4.3 Class 3 — Building Conceptual Bridges
Key domains: Numbers to 1,000+, Multiplication, Division, Fractions, Full measurement, Data handling, Geometry.
Most Advanced: Tamil Nadu (to 9,999), Kerala (constructivist approach to all domains).
Most FLN-Aligned: Kerala (9/10), Tamil Nadu (9/10)
Strongest Problem-Solving: Tamil Nadu — dedicated 'Try This', 'Think and Do', and 'Project' sections in all chapters.
Strongest Activity-Based: Mizoram — Maths Fest design; Kerala — investigative activities throughout.
Gap: Division is explicitly taught in only 70% of states at Class 3; 30% defer to Class 4. Data handling charts appear in fewer than 50% of states' Class 3 books.


4.4 Class 4 — Preparing for Mathematical Thinking
Key domains: Large numbers (to 10,000+), All four operations, Fractions with operations, Geometry (area/perimeter), Data interpretation, Money, Time.
Most Advanced: Tamil Nadu (to 99,999; area and perimeter; complex fractions).
Most FLN-Aligned: Kerala (9/10), Tamil Nadu (9/10)
Most Comprehensive Geometry: Tamil Nadu and Kerala — cover angles, symmetry, triangles, quadrilaterals, area, perimeter.
Weakest at Class 4: Bihar — limited fraction work, basic data handling, no area/perimeter.
Gap: 'Algebraic thinking' (pattern generalisation, finding unknowns) is present only in Kerala and Tamil Nadu at Class 4; completely absent in 80% of states.

	6. FLN Alignment Analysis
6.1 NIPUN Bharat Benchmarks vs State Curricula
The NIPUN Bharat mission (2021) sets specific competency benchmarks for Grade 3 completion:
      * Numbers: Count, read, write numbers up to 999
      * Addition and Subtraction: 2- and 3-digit numbers with and without regrouping
      * Multiplication: Multiplication tables up to 5 × 5
      * Division: Simple equal distribution (up to 2-digit ÷ 1-digit)
      * Fractions: Identify halves and quarters
      * Measurement: Length (cm/m), Weight (g/kg), Capacity (mL/L)
      * Geometry: Identify 2D and 3D shapes
      * Data: Read simple pictographs and bar charts


Assessment of states against NIPUN Grade 3 targets:
      * Exceeds NIPUN (ahead of benchmarks): Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, Mizoram, AP, Telangana, Karnataka
      * Meets NIPUN (on target): Gujarat, West Bengal, Odisha, Assam, Punjab, UP, Jharkhand, Rajasthan, and all NCERT-following states
      * Partially meets NIPUN (some gaps): Bihar, Chhattisgarh, MP (in activity and data handling components)


6.2 NEP 2020 Pedagogical Alignment
NEP 2020 emphasises: play-based and activity-based learning, discovery and exploration, real-life contexts, multiple representations, inclusion, and reducing content load in favour of depth. Against these principles:
      * Kerala: Fully aligned — the national benchmark for NEP 2020 pedagogy at primary level
      * Tamil Nadu: Highly aligned — activity components strong, real-life contexts excellent
      * Mizoram: Highly aligned — 'Joyful Mathematics' exemplifies NEP spirit
      * Maharashtra, AP, Telangana, Karnataka: Well aligned — good balance
      * NCERT states: Moderately aligned — Math-Magic series incorporates NEP principles but real-life contexts are generic
      * Bihar, some MP content: Needs improvement — procedure-first without conceptual foundation

8. Key Findings
8.1 National Patterns
      * The majority of states (62%) are classified as 'Average' — adequate NIPUN compliance but limited innovation in pedagogy.
      * Activity-based learning is the single most differentiating factor between high and low FLN scores.
      * Data handling is the most neglected domain — absent or minimal in 45% of states at Classes 1–2.
      * Fractions are introduced too late in most states (Class 4); Tamil Nadu and Kerala introduce them in Class 3.
      * Mental mathematics is systematically developed in fewer than 30% of state curricula.
      * Division is introduced in Class 4 in over one-third of states — behind the NIPUN benchmark of Class 3.


8.2 Regional Patterns
Region
	States
	Key Pattern
	South India
	Kerala, Tamil Nadu, AP, Telangana, Karnataka
	Highest FLN scores, strongest activity base, advanced concept progression
	West India
	Maharashtra, Gujarat
	Above average; strong conceptual design
	Northeast India
	Mizoram, Assam, Manipur, Meghalaya, Arunachal, Nagaland, Tripura, Sikkim
	Mizoram stands out; rest follow NCERT
	East India
	West Bengal, Odisha, Jharkhand, Bihar
	Mixed; WB/Odisha moderate; Bihar weakest nationally
	North India
	Punjab, Haryana, HP, J&K, Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, UP, MP, CG
	Average; most follow NCERT; UP improving
	

8.3 Curriculum Design Trends
      * Semester/term structure is associated with better content pacing and depth (Kerala, TN, Mizoram, Karnataka, AP Classes 1–2)
      * States with dedicated SCERT textbooks consistently outperform NCERT-only adopters on FLN metrics
      * Cultural contextualisation is positively correlated with FLN alignment — Mizoram, Kerala, AP all use community contexts effectively
      * Place value is universally covered but depth varies dramatically — Kerala uses proportional reasoning while many states only use digits and positions
9. Recommendations
9.1 For State Curriculum Designers
1. Adopt Activity-First Design:
All states should redesign Classes 1–2 textbooks to lead with hands-on, contextual activities before introducing abstract notation. Kerala's model is directly replicable.
2. Introduce Data Handling by Class 2:
Simple pictographs and tally charts should be explicitly taught by end of Class 2 in all states. Tamil Nadu and AP demonstrate how to do this effectively.
3. Culturalise the NCERT Curriculum:
States following NCERT without adaptation should develop state-specific context supplements  — word problems set in local geography, festivals, and occupations.
4. Advance Fraction Introduction:
Fractions (half, quarter, third) should appear as activity-based exploration in Class 3, not as purely procedural content in Class 4.
5. Strengthen Mental Mathematics:
Every class should include a dedicated mental math section with estimation, number bonds, and mental computation strategies.
9.2 For NIPUN Bharat Implementation Teams
      * Use Kerala and Tamil Nadu curricula as exemplars for FLN training materials
      * Develop a national activity bank that can be customised by each state
      * Establish clear benchmarks for data handling and fractions at Class 2–3 level
      * Support Bihar, Chhattisgarh, and MP to update their Class 1–2 textbooks with urgency
9.3 For FLN Program Planners
      * Teacher training is the most critical variable — Kerala's excellent curriculum underperforms when teachers lack constructivist pedagogy skills
      * Assessment alignment: ensure formative assessment tools reflect FLN competencies, not just textbook exercises
      * Bridge materials are needed for NCERT states to close the gap with high-performing southern states
10. Final State Rankings — Composite Score
Composite score = weighted average of FLN Alignment (50%) + Curriculum Rigor (30%) + Activity Level (20%) across all four classes.

Initiative for Foundational Literacy and Numeracy (FLN) :
1. NIPUN Bharat - (National Initiative for Proficiency in Reading with Understanding and Numeracy)
NIPUN Bharat — the National Initiative for Proficiency in Reading with Understanding and Numeracy — was launched on 5th July 2021 by the Department of School Education and Literacy under the Ministry of Education. Its vision is to create an enabling environment to ensure the universal acquisition of foundational literacy and numeracy, so that every child achieves the desired learning competencies in reading, writing, and numeracy by the end of Grade 3, by 2026–27. 
The NIPUN Bharat initiative focuses on several primary components:
      * Establishing FLN targets specific to each state
      * Enhancing teacher training
      * Ensuring school readiness
      * Implementing digital tracking systems
  

  

  

  

Foundational Numeracy(pg 84):
Foundational Numeracy means the ability to reason and to apply simple numerical concepts in daily life problem solving. When children acquire the following skills, it is said that they have developed number sense and spatial understanding.
 It includes the ability to:
i. Make an understanding of quantities
ii. Develop concepts like more and less, and larger and smaller
iii. Establish relationships between single items and groups of items (seven means one group of seven items which is one more than a group of six items).
iv. Use symbols that represent quantities (7 means the same thing as seven).
v. Compare numbers (10 is greater than 8, and three is half of six).
vi. Arrange numbers in a list in order: 1st, 2nd, 3rd, etc.
vii. Visualise shapes and space around them



Children grow up counting their toys, toffees, people at home or other small sets of objects. They are often asked who has more/less or are there enough objects in their regular conversations. So, when schools begin to develop the understanding of pre-number concept, they should build upon the child’s experience from his/her familiar context.
 
Classification involves putting together things that have some characteristics in common. So, when organising tasks on classification, we must make sure that the activities are meaningful to them and, they are familiar with the objects which she/he mustclassify.Initiallychildrenshouldbeencouragedtoclassifyonone property/characteristic only. Gradually, complexity of the task should be increased where they can classify on more than one property such as colour, size, shape etc. This will later help them in understanding number sense.


Seriation involves ordering a set of objects according to some rule. Intrinsically, it also involves ordering objects in two directions. For example, the child applies the relations ‘bigger than’ and ‘smaller than’ at the same time. It also means understanding the logic of transitivity which means that if A is more than B and B is more than C, then A is also more than C. Seriation also forms the base for understanding of patterns. Thus, it should be build using the objects from the familiar contexts of children and initially using 3 objects only. Gradually, children should be presented with more objects to seriate.


One-to-one correspondence involves matching or pairing of objects. For building upon the understanding of one-to-one correspondence, children need to understand the meaning of ‘many and few’, ‘more than/ less than’ and ‘as many as’. Teachers need to design tasks contextual to child’s context so that the child relates and uses them in daily life experiences. 


.2. Vidya Pravesh - A 3-month school readiness program developed by NCERT for children entering Grade 1.
That includes play based learning , pre-literacy , pre-numeracy and communication activities.
3.NISHTHA FLN-
NISHTHA FLN is a teacher training programme designed to help teachers understand:
      * how children learn in early grades,
      * how to teach reading and numeracy effectively,
      * activity-based learning methods,
      * child psychology,
      * assessment techniques,
      * inclusive classrooms,
      * competency-based education.
Child Psychology Points in NISHTHA FLN
1. Brain Development & Neuroscience
      * Over 85% of a child's cumulative brain development occurs before age 6 [making early years the most critical window for care and stimulation.
      * The brain is most flexible and adaptable to learning during early childhood.
      * Rapid brain development in early years means children need a nutrient-dense diet; deficiencies in protein, vitamins, and minerals can cause delayed mental and motor development with lasting effects.
      * Positive early experiences support lifelong learning and development; learning gaps, if unfilled early, continue to widen.
________________


2. Cognitive Development
Children vary in cognitive abilities and styles — they think, analyse, and make decisions differently.
Cognitive development includes classification, seriation, matching, sorting, sequential thinking, and one-to-one correspondence.
Children move from perception-bound thinking to concept-based understanding through direct experience with physical, social, and natural environments.
Ex. - A child may memorize “3 + 2 = 5,” but true numeracy develops when the child can actually see, touch, compare, group, and use quantities in real life .
Instead of directly directly giving 4>2 give 4 stone and 2 stone 
Mathematical thinking (quantitative and spatial reasoning) is a key subdomain of cognitive development, built through meaningful activities.
Ex.- Arranging five objects on the basis of their size.( Make the question like that student have ability of mathematical thinking )
Information processing — children visualise text and create mental images to comprehend it (schema-based memory).
If a child imagines:
🍎🍎🍎 + 🍎🍎
           they mentally visualize quantity before solving:
            3 + 2 = 5
           This improves number sense and problem-solving.
Age and psychological maturity directly affect reading ability.
            A 3-year-old child may enjoy pictures and listening to stories
             A 6-year-old child may  identify letter ,read simple words
Multilingualism is linked positively to cognitive flexibility and scholastic achievement.
________________


3. Socio-Emotional Development
      * Early childhood is described as a critical period for social and emotional development (equated with mental health).
ex.-A calm child can carefully count objects and identify mistakes more easily.
      * Socio-emotional development includes: experience and management of emotions, ability to form positive relationships, and ability to explore the environment.
      * Positive socio-emotional development builds self-confidence, empathy, meaningful friendships, and a sense of personal value.
      * Socio-emotional development influences all other areas of development.
      * The quality of early experiences lays either a strong or weak foundation for how children respond to the world for the rest of their lives.
      * Children need to develop pro-social behaviours: caring, sharing, helping, cooperating.
________________


4. Self-Concept & Self-Regulation
      * Children need to develop a positive self-concept — they must be encouraged and admired so their self-image grows healthily.
      * Self-esteem develops through autonomy, confidence in growing abilities, and achievements nurtured from early childhood.
      * Self-regulation — the ability to focus, refocus, and manage distractions -is identified as a critical competency. Yoga, meditation, visualisation, and breathing techniques are recommended as age-old practices to develop it.
      * Using a language children don't understand can damage their identity, self-confidence, and self-esteem, leading to negative academic outcomes.
________________


5. Motivation & Learning Approaches
      * There is a strong research link between a child's interest and their motivation, achievement, productivity, and perseverance.
      * Intrinsic motivation is activated when classroom content connects to children's personal interests and goals.
      * Children should be encouraged to become inquisitive, initiative-taking, confident, innovative, and reflective learners.
Ex.- A child is learning counting and says:
“1, 2, 3, 5…”
            instead of feeling embarrassed, the teacher responds:
  “Very good! You counted carefully till 3. Let’s find the next number together.
      * Confidence is particularly important for fearlessness and happiness in learning.
________________


6. Learning Styles & Individual Differences
      * Children have different modality preferences: visual, auditory, kinaesthetic, or tactual — the majority prefer visual learning.
Example:
🍎🍎🍎 + 🍎🍎 is easier than only “3 + 2”. (visual learning improves number understanding)
jumping while counting,arranging blocks,sorting object -> improves mathematical concept
Rhymes and songs help children remember number sequences ,tables      patterns and counting order
      * Environmental preferences (time of day, temperature, seating) also affect learning performance.
      * Grouping preferences — some children work best alone, others prefer collaborative settings.
      * Competency-based education matches instruction to the child's developmental readiness, not age or time — children move forward only upon mastery.
________________


7. Play & Learning
      * Play is the primary mode of learning in early childhood — it supports physical, emotional, cognitive, and linguistic development simultaneously.
      * Through play children develop social and emotional skills, self-control (waiting for turns, following rules), and problem-solving.
      * Pretend play and role play develop imagination and social understanding.
      * Curiosity, initiative, and perseverance develop through exploratory and discovery-based play.
________________


8. Social Learning & Relationships
      * Children learn social skills by watching, mimicking, and interacting with adults and peers.
      * Parents and caregivers have the biggest role in social/emotional development as they offer the most consistent relationships.
      * Consistent relationships with family, teachers, and adults help children understand emotions in predictable interactions.
      * Learning is described as an active, collaborative, and social process — interaction between teacher–child, material–child, and child–child is essential.
      * Children internalise activities, habits, behaviours, and ideas from their social context (aligned with Vygotskian social learning principles, though not named explicitly).
________________


9. Secure Attachment & Early Environment
      * Children who have secure attachments, safety, good nutrition, and enriched learning opportunities in early years are better prepared for school.
      * Children from less fortunate early environments show differences in school readiness.
      * A warm, nurturing, safe environment is essential — children must feel relaxed and connected with peers.
      * Children need to be protected from emotional and psychological distress, and taught about good touch/bad touch.
________________


10. Fear, Anxiety & Stress in Learning
      * A fear-free classroom environment is repeatedly emphasised — fear hinders participation, confidence, and expression.
      * Mathematics anxiety is explicitly mentioned and should be avoided through peer learning, collaborative problem-solving, and teacher support.
      * Self-assessment is preferred over third-party assessment as it avoids the child feeling labelled or stressed.
      * Children should learn in a stress-free atmosphere where they can express thoughts and feelings freely.
________________


11. Language, Identity & Psychology
      * A child's home/mother tongue is tied to their identity and emotional wellbeing — teaching in an unfamiliar language leads to feelings of helplessness and low self-esteem.
      * Mixed language use (translanguaging) supports meaning-making, understanding, and identity affirmation.
      * A fear-free linguistic environment is needed — children should never be pressured to learn a second language too quickly.


4. DIKSHA -
DIKSHA platform provides all these features for students from Classes 1 to 12. It is a national digital learning platform launched by NCERT and the Ministry of Education, Government of India.
Some important features of DIKSHA are:
      * Audio learning content
      * Educational videos
      * E-textbooks and PDFs
      * Interactive quizzes and assessments
      * Practice questions and worksheets
      * QR-code based textbook learning
      * Multilingual support
      * Teacher training courses
      * Offline content access
5. PARAKH-
PARAKH evaluates students based on understanding, problem-solving, reading, numeracy, critical thinking, and real-life application of learning instead of only memorization. It also focuses on holistic development, including communication, creativity, and participation. 
Class 3 PARAKH test pdf
6. Jadui Pitara
Jaadui Pitara is an NCERT play-based learning kit for children aged 3–8 years. It includes toys, games, storybooks, and activities to make learning joyful, interactive, and skill-based according to NEP 2020’s Foundational Stage. 
7.ASER (2024)
ASER(2023)
ASER(2022)
8.NAS (National Achievement Survey)
      * Conducted by PARAKH (Performance Assessment, Review, and Analysis of Knowledge for Holistic Development), which operates under the NCERT (National Council of Educational Research and Training) and the Ministry of Education, Government of India. 
      * Large-scale assessment of students
      * Covers Classes 3, 5, 8, and 10
      * Measures learning outcomes in Language, Mathematics, Science, etc.
      * Provides state-wise and national learning data.
Learning outcomes for class 3
  

9. FLS (Foundational Learning Study)
      * Conducted by NCERT in 2022
      * Focuses specifically on Class 3 FLN skills
      * Assesses reading fluency, reading comprehension, and numeracy
      * Conducted in about 20 languages with around 86,000 students
      * Created to establish FLN benchmarks under NIPUN Bharat.
  

The study targets Grade 3 students across 10,000+ schools in all 36 States/UTs. Up to 12 students per school are selected through a systematic random sampling process — using the school's PIN code to generate a random start, then selecting every nth student from the class register.
________________


How the Assessment is Conducted
The assessment is one-on-one, oral, and tablet-based. A trained Field Investigator (FI) sits with each child individually and administers the test using a digital app (developed by IIT Delhi). The child responds verbally or by pointing/touching the screen — they are never coached, prompted, or penalized.
10. EAC-PM FLN Report
      * Published under the guidance of the Economic Advisory Council to the Prime Minister (EAC-PM)
      * Does not conduct assessments itself
      * Uses data from NAS and FLS
      * Creates the FLN Index and state rankings
      * Analyzes why some states perform better or worse in foundational learning.
Based on NEP 2020, the EAC-PM/Institute for Competitiveness FLN report, and ASER 2024, here's a structured picture of where India stands.
EGMA = Early Grade Mathematics Assessment
      *  Developed by: RTI International + USAID
      * Year: 2009
      * Used in: 60+ countries
      * Purpose: Directly measure if young children 
         (Grade 1-3) actually understand 
         basic mathematics
      * Method: One-on-one oral assessment
        Child responds verbally or by pointing
        No writing required


UNICEF :United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund 
Aspect
	Details
	Who conducts it?
	UNICEF through the Foundational Learning Skills (FLS) Module, usually implemented in household surveys such as MICS.
	Assessment Matrix
	Reading accuracy, reading comprehension, inferential comprehension, number identification, number comparison, addition, pattern recognition.
	Evaluation Metric
	Whether children achieve foundational literacy and numeracy skills expected by the end of early primary grades. UNICEF also uses the concept of Learning Poverty (unable to read and understand a simple text by age 10).
	Age Group
	Mainly children aged 7–14 years.
	Global Benchmark
	Child should be able to read with comprehension and perform basic numeracy tasks.
	Major Gaps Found
1. Schooling vs Learning Gap
Children attend school but do not acquire foundational skills. This is the biggest gap highlighted by UNICEF and UNESCO.
2. Reading Comprehension Gap
Many children can decode words but cannot understand what they read.
3. Numeracy Gap
Students struggle with basic number sense, comparison, and arithmetic operations.
4. Equity Gap
Learning outcomes vary by:
      * Rural vs Urban
      * Rich vs Poor
      * Gender
      * Language background
5. Early Childhood Gap
Children who miss quality pre-primary education are less likely to achieve FLN competencies later.
TIMSS:
TIMSS is the world's largest and longest-running international assessment of Mathematics and Science achievement.
 Full name : Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study 
Run by : IEA (International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement)
 First held : 1995
 Frequency : Every 4 years (1995, 1999, 2003, 2007, 2011, 2015, 2019, 2023) Grades : Grade 4 (age ~10) and Grade 8 (age ~14)
 Subjects : Mathematics + Science 
Countries : 64 countries + 6 benchmarking systems (TIMSS 2023) 
Students : 650,000+ students assessed globally (TIMSS 2023)
 Format : Digital assessment (since 2019); 
fully digital 2023 SDG link : Reports on SDG 4.1.1 (b) and (c) via GPF linking

	NOTE: 'Low' (400) is the minimum baseline, NOT the MPL. The GPF 'Meets MPL' (Level 3) roughly corresponds to TIMSS Intermediate (475) benchmark.
SDG 4 — QUALITY EDUCATION United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 4 "Ensure inclusive and equitable quality education and promote lifelong learning opportunities for all" by 2030 
SDG 4 is one of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals adopted in 2015 by all 193 UN member countries (including India) as part of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. It is the global commitment that every person on Earth should have access to quality education — from early childhood to adulthood. 
- India signed the 2030 Agenda and is committed to all SDG 4 targets - Key programs aligned to SDG 4: 
• NIPUN Bharat Mission (foundational literacy & numeracy) • National Education Policy 2020 (NEP 2020) 
• Samagra Shiksha (school education scheme) 
• PARAKH (national assessment body under Ministry of Education) - Current gaps:
 • 56% of children at end of primary are Learning Poor (reading) • India has NOT reported SDG 4.1.1 data in the last 4 years 
• National assessments not yet formally linked to GPF scale 






Grade
	Reading Skills
	Numeracy Skills
	Grade 1
	Letter recognition, phonics, word decoding
	Number recognition, counting, simple addition
	Grade 2
	Word reading, sentence comprehension
	2-digit operations, basic shapes
	Grade 3 (MPL Gate)
	Short text reading, literal comprehension
	3-digit operations, fractions intro
	Grade 4
	Paragraph comprehension, vocabulary in context
	Fractions, measurement, simple data reading
	Grade 5 (MPL Gate)
	Multi-paragraph reading, inferential comprehension
	Multi-step problems, bar charts, perimeter/area
	

India does not participate in any international large-scale assessment (like PIRLS or PASEC) that is officially linked to the GPF. India uses its own National Learning Assessment (NLA), which is run by PARAKH under the Ministry of Education. Because India's test is not formally mapped to the GPF, its data has limited global comparability — the World Bank uses it as a best-available proxy but notes this as a significant data gap.

	Sampark Foundation:  Sampark Foundation is a prominent, outcomes-driven education non-profit in India dedicated to transforming primary school classrooms. Founded in 2005 by Anupama Nayar and Vineet Nayar (former Vice Chairman and CEO of HCL Technologies), the foundation operates on the philosophy of "frugal innovation" to improve foundational learning at scale 
Sampark relies on structured pedagogy ("Sahi Kram, Sahi Dhang") and practical, offline-ready technology: [1, 2, 3, 4]
      * Sampark Smart Shala Program: An integrated intervention combining structured FLN pedagogy, physical "teach-easy" kits, and smart-classroom setups. It breaks down complex math and English into activity-based learning. [1, 2, 3, 4]
      * Sampark AI Smart Shala App: A highly popular application providing state-textbook-aligned lesson plans, animated videos, interactive games, and automated assessments. It serves as a non-judgmental training ground where teachers can access mock classroom demonstrations. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
      * Sampark TV: A hardware innovation that converts standard, non-smart television sets in rural classrooms into interactive teaching tools without requiring constant internet connectivity. 
World Bank : Foundational Literacy and Numeracy (FLN) Initiatives in IndiaCore Pedagogical & Structural Strategies
      * Teaching at the Right Level (TaRL): Groups children by current learning level rather than grade. Originally developed by Pratham; now widely used for early grade remediation.
      * NIPUN Bharat: Mandates a shift to joyful, play-based pedagogy (storytelling, games, puzzles, puppets). Includes "Vidya Pravesh," a 3-month school readiness module.
      * Multilingual Instruction: NEP 2020 emphasizes teaching in the child's home language to prevent learning gaps (e.g., Odisha's tribal MTB-MLE program, J&K’s Urdu/Punjabi initiatives).
      * Digital Learning & EdTech: The DIKSHA platform hosts multilingual content (videos, QR-linked textbooks). State-specific apps are used for monitoring:
      * UP: Gunvatta App & Prerna Portal
      * Telangana: Telangana Education App
      * Bihar: Tangerine
      * Structured Pedagogy: Prescribes dedicated, protected FLN time blocks (e.g., UP mandates 120 minutes daily).
      * Community Engagement:
      * Tamil Nadu: Illam Thedi Kalvi (volunteers teaching after school).
      * Karnataka: FLN Melas and community recognition events.
      * Nutrition Integration: "Poshan Bhi, Padhai Bhi" (2023–24) reorients Anganwadis to combine nutrition services with structured early learning.
      * Teacher Training: NISHTHA FLN (12-module program on DIKSHA). States use monthly learning circles and e-magazines (e.g., Bihar’s NIPUN Samvad) for peer coaching.
      * Formative Assessment: Replaces annual exams with low-stakes, frequent checks (e.g., UP’s thrice-yearly Prerna assessments, Telangana’s baseline-midline-endline tracking).

4. Key findings & challenges
Literacy-numeracy gap: Only 30.7% of Class 5 students can solve a basic division problem vs. 48.7% who read fluently — an 18 percentage point gap (ASER 2024). No state reports higher numeracy than literacy.
Top performers: UP's Mission Prerna (launched 2019, pre-NIPUN) demonstrates how early, mission-mode reform with top-down accountability and real-time monitoring (Gunvatta App) delivers results even in large, low-resource states.
Unexpected underperformers: Many southern states — traditionally higher on human development indicators — have not matched the gains seen in UP, Bihar, and Himachal Pradesh.
Marginalised groups: Grade 3 students from pucca houses are roughly twice as likely to read at grade level vs. those in katcha homes. Girls whose mothers attended school show 2.2-2.4x higher literacy levels.
NIPUN Bharat target: Universal FLN by Grade 3 by 2026-27. While recovery trends are positive, absolute proficiency remains low — only 1 in 4 Grade 3 students reads at grade level in government schools.
 
Sources: ASER 2024 National Findings | Ideas for India (April 2026) | NIPUN Bharat MoE Dashboard | Karnataka FLN Reach Program (UNESCO SDG4 Knowledge Hub) | IFET Arunachal Pradesh | Anantam IAS FLN Index Report

SDG 4 — QUALITY EDUCATION United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 4 "Ensure inclusive and equitable quality education and promote lifelong learning opportunities for all" by 2030 
SDG 4 is one of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals adopted in 2015 by all 193 UN member countries (including India) as part of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. It is the global commitment that every person on Earth should have access to quality education — from early childhood to adulthood. 
- India signed the 2030 Agenda and is committed to all SDG 4 targets - Key programs aligned to SDG 4: • NIPUN Bharat Mission (foundational literacy & numeracy) • National Education Policy 2020 (NEP 2020) • Samagra Shiksha (school education scheme) • PARAKH (national assessment body under Ministry of Education) - Current gaps: • 56% of children at end of primary are Learning Poor (reading) • India has NOT reported SDG 4.1.1 data in the last 4 years • National assessments not yet formally linked to GPF scale 


India does not participate in any international large-scale assessment (like PIRLS or PASEC) that is officially linked to the GPF. India uses its own National Learning Assessment (NLA), which is run by PARAKH under the Ministry of Education. Because India's test is not formally mapped to the GPF, its data has limited global comparability — the World Bank uses it as a best-available proxy but notes this as a significant data gap. 


1. Why FLN matters
FLN — basic reading with comprehension and basic arithmetic — is treated as the precondition for all later learning, not just one more skill. NEP 2020 is explicit about this: attaining foundational literacy and numeracy for all children is described as an urgent national mission, with the rest of the policy only becoming relevant once this basic learning requirement is achieved. Shikshan
The EAC-PM report frames the stakes in human and economic terms. When children cannot read, write, and understand simple text, they struggle to learn anything else in school, which makes them more likely to repeat grades and drop out — outcomes that cascade into poor health, high youth unemployment, and entrenched poverty. It also cites UNICEF's stark global figure that nearly two-thirds of 10-year-olds worldwide cannot read and understand a simple text. eacpmeacpm
The pandemic sharpened the urgency. In low- and middle-income countries, the share of 10-year-olds unable to read and understand simple text jumped from 57% to 70% between 2019 and 2022, and India alone saw roughly 250 million students affected by school closures, with 92% of Indian students losing at least one language ability and 82% losing at least one math ability relative to the prior year, per UNICEF 2022 data. The report also notes the global economic cost: school closures put the current generation at risk of $17 trillion in lifetime earnings, equivalent to 14% of global GDP. eacpm + 3
2. How India measures FLN
Three main instruments are used:
National Achievement Survey (NAS) — run periodically since 2001, testing Language, Mathematics, and Environmental Studies at Grades 3, 5, 8, and 10. NAS 2017 sampled around 1,10,000 schools and 2,20,00,000 students, while NAS 2021 expanded to roughly 1,18,274 schools, 5,26,824 teachers, and 34,01,158 students. eacpm
Foundational Learning Study (FLS) 2022 — a more FLN-specific assessment. Conducted by the Ministry of Education and NCERT with UNICEF support, it tested over 86,000 Grade 3 students in 20 languages across 10,000 schools, using the Global Proficiency Framework with four bands — Below Partially Meets, Partially Meets, Meets, and Exceeds global minimum proficiency. eacpm
ASER (Annual Status of Education Report) — Pratham/ASER Centre's annual rural household survey. It assesses basic reading and arithmetic abilities of children aged 5–16, and for the first time in 2024, also measured digital literacy among 14–16-year-olds. ASER 2024 reached 649,491 children across 17,997 villages in 605 rural districts. ASER popularized the "Teaching at the Right Level" approach — grouping children by actual ability rather than grade. AsercentreMillenniumPost
The Index on Foundational Learning (EAC-PM) — a composite benchmarking tool covering 36 indicators across five domains: Educational Infrastructure, Access to Education, Basic Health, Learning Outcomes, and Governance, built using Principal Component Analysis and used to rank states/UTs against each other. eacpm

      * A simple difficulty progression for skip-counting questions is 
Easy : Pattern recognition and forward counting. 
             2s: 2,4,6,8 , __, __
            3s: 3, 6, 9, 12 , __
             4s : 4, 8, 12, 16 , __
Medium: Pattern detection, repeated addition, working memory. 
                 Ex.-  4, __ , 12 , __ , 16
                           6 , 9 , 12 , __
                            8, 12, 16 , __
Tough: Working memory, mental arithmetic, logical reasoning, and transfer of learning. 
             Ex.- 10 , 8 , 6 , __
                       A frog jumps 7 spaces each time. It starts at 14. Where will it          be after 8 jumps? 
      * Yes/No Questions are Easy :
 According to child development theories (e.g., Jean Piaget):
      * Children only need to recognize information, not generate an answer.
      * They require less working memory and cognitive effort.
      * They reduce language load, helping children focus on the mathematical concept.
      * Young children feel more confident because they have only two response options.
      Demerit: A child can guess correctly with a 50% chance. 
In child psychology, Yes/No questions are not very effective for determining a child's behavior, thinking process, or mathematical understanding. They mainly show whether a child can recognize something.

Number sense and cognitive lo
 


	Cognitive Load Analysis
Cognitive load refers to the amount of mental effort required to solve a question.
Children have limited working memory, so questions requiring more information processing become difficult even if the concept is known.
A child may fail a question not because they don't know the concept, but because:
      * Working memory gets overloaded.
      * Too many visual elements are present.
      * Multiple steps are required.
      * The question format is unfamiliar.
Therefore, in FLN assessment, difficulty should be measured not only by the mathematical concept but also by the cognitive load required to solve the question.
      * Addition with carry:




      * Subtraction  with Borrow:
Types of  questions ;
Easy ; simple subtraction  ex-42-18
Moderate :Multiple borrowing ex-72 − 48 
Hard- Borrowing across zero ex- 50-27
Very hard : missing number  ex- __ -   18=24  and Word problem 


Error Type
	Example
	Explanation
	Subtracting larger from smaller without borrowing
	42 − 18 = 34
	The student subtracts the smaller digit from the larger digit in each column, failing to regroup/borrow.
	Forgetting to reduce tens after borrowing
	52 − 18 = 44
	The student borrows correctly but fails to decrease the value in the tens place, treating it as unchanged.
	Confusion with zeros
	302 − 145
	Difficulty regrouping when zeros are present; the student often treats the zero as a dead end or an un-borrowable placeholder.
	Misalignment of place values
	125 − 34 = 91 (aligned incorrectly)
	Poor spatial organization leading to column shifts; digits are not aligned by place value, causing incorrect calculations.
	





   




































Why Comparison as the Foundation of Numeracy
Jean Piaget, in The Child's Conception of Number (1941), showed that children's relationship with numbers starts with one simple skill: comparison. Before a child can count, order, or conserve quantity, they can already tell which pile has more, which glass has less, or when two things look the same. Every higher numerical skill — sequencing, counting, understanding that quantity doesn't change when you rearrange objects — builds on this basic ability to compare.
This is why comparison-based tasks are the right starting point for any foundational numeracy intervention.

SECTION 1: BACKGROUND AND POLICY FRAMEWORK
	 
1. Background and Policy Context
Foundational Literacy and Numeracy (FLN) refers to the ability of a child to read with comprehension and perform basic arithmetic operations by the time they complete Grade 3 (approximately age 8-9). It constitutes the bedrock of all future learning and has long-lasting implications for the child's education, health, and economic outcomes.
 
According to the World Bank, approximately 50% of children in India lack foundational learning. By Grade 5, these gaps become entrenched and extremely difficult to bridge.
	 
1.1 The NIPUN Bharat Mission
The Government of India launched the National Initiative for Proficiency in Reading with Understanding and Numeracy (NIPUN Bharat) on 5th July 2021. The mission envisions that every child in India will achieve desired foundational literacy and numeracy competencies by the end of Grade 3 — covering children aged 3 to 9 years — by 2026-27.
 
•       Implemented under the Samagra Shiksha scheme (umbrella programme for school education)
•       Budget for Samagra Shiksha increased by 20% from Rs. 31,050 crores (2021-22) to Rs. 37,383 crores
•       NCERT is responsible for developing learning materials, defining outcomes, and building teacher capacity
•       Every Anganwadi Centre and foundational-grade school must follow the NIPUN framework
•       Emphasizes mother tongue / home language as the medium of instruction during foundational years
•       Promotes activity-based, discovery, and play-based teaching methods
•       Each state is required to formulate its own FLN action plan
 
1.2 National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 and FLN
NEP 2020 is the driving policy document behind FLN reforms in India. Key provisions directly relevant to FLN include:
 
•       Strong emphasis on Early Childhood Care and Education (ECCE) as a critical foundation
•       Home/local language mandated as medium of instruction at least until Grade 5, preferably until Grade 8
•       Three-language formula made flexible — no language can be imposed on any state
•       Foundational stage (age 3-8) treated as a distinct curricular phase requiring specialized pedagogy
•       25% of primary school children face a moderate to severe learning disadvantage because classroom language differs from home language (World Bank, 2021)
 
1.3 Key Assessment Frameworks
India uses two primary large-scale assessment tools to track FLN progress:
 
Assessment
	Conducted by
	Scope
	Grade/Age Focus
	ASER (Annual Status of Education Report)
	Pratham (NGO)
	Rural household survey, all districts
	Age 3-16, all grades
	NAS (National Achievement Survey)
	NCERT / Ministry of Education
	School-based, census sample
	Grade 3, 5, 8, 10
	FLS (Foundational Learning Study) 2022
	MoE + NCERT + UNICEF

	Grade 3 students only
	 
 


 
SECTION 2: ENROLLMENT AND ACCESS DATA (ASER 2024)
	 
2. Enrollment and Access to Education
2.1 Pre-Primary Enrollment (Age 3-5)
ASER 2024 reveals significant improvements in pre-primary enrollment, a critical enabler of FLN outcomes. Children who attend quality pre-primary programmes arrive at Grade 1 with stronger foundational skills.
 
Age Group
	Enrollment in Pre-Primary 2018
	Enrollment 2022
	Enrollment 2024
	3-year-olds
	68.1%
	75.8%
	77.4%
	4-year-olds
	76.0%
	82.0%
	83.4%
	5-year-olds
	58.4%
	62.1%
	71.4%
	 
•       Anganwadi centres remain the dominant provider — over 50% of all 3 and 4-year-olds are enrolled in Anganwadis
•       States achieving near-universal pre-primary enrollment (>95%) for 4-year-olds: Gujarat, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Odisha
•       Lagging states (over 50% of 3-year-olds not enrolled anywhere): Meghalaya and Uttar Pradesh
•       Proportion of 'underage' children (age 5 or below) in Std I dropped from 25.6% (2018) to 16.7% (2024) — lowest ever recorded
 
2.2 Elementary School Enrollment (Age 6-14)
India has achieved near-universal enrollment in the 6-14 age group, a remarkable feat sustained for nearly two decades. However, the shift between government and private schooling is noteworthy.

	2022
	2024

	Enrolled in Government Schools

	~30%
	~25%
	~29%
	 
The pandemic caused a large temporary shift to government schools (72.9% in 2022), but by 2024, private school enrollment has partially recovered. This is seen across virtually every state except Uttarakhand and Jammu & Kashmir.

SECTION 3: FLN IMPLEMENTATION IN SCHOOLS
	 
3. FLN Programme Implementation Status
3.1 School-Level FLN Activities (ASER 2024)
ASER 2024 gathered data from government primary school teachers and principals on FLN programme implementation. Results show high levels of government directive compliance but ongoing gaps in delivery quality.

These are encouraging signs at the input level. However, input availability does not automatically translate to learning outcomes, as classroom observations and the learning data in Section 4 demonstrate.
 
3.2 State-Led FLN Initiatives (EAC-PM FLN Report)
Beyond the central NIPUN Bharat framework, several states have designed their own FLN programmes to address specific gaps:
 
•       Andhra Pradesh: Mana Badi Nadu Nedu — infrastructure improvement alongside FLN pedagogy
•       Uttar Pradesh: Mission Prerna — structured FLN curriculum, with major learning recovery shown in ASER 2024
•       Kerala: High teacher training investment; among top performers in reading levels
•       Himachal Pradesh: Among the highest-performing states in Std V and VIII reading levels
•       Gujarat: Near-universal pre-primary enrollment; strong public-private partnership in FLN delivery
•       Punjab: Consistent top performer in both literacy and numeracy across grades

SECTION 6: FOUNDATIONAL LEARNING STUDY (FLS) 2022 — NATIONAL ASSESSMENT
	 
6. FLS 2022 National Assessment Findings
The Foundational Learning Study (FLS) 2022 was the first of its kind in India — a school-based assessment of Grade 3 students conducted in 20 different languages across approximately 10,000 schools, covering over 86,000 students. It used the Global Proficiency Framework (GPF) as its benchmark.
 
6.1 Global Proficiency Levels Explained

	Limited knowledge; can partially complete basic grade-level tasks
	Meets (M)
	Sufficient knowledge; can successfully complete basic grade-level tasks
	Exceeds (E)
	Superior knowledge; can complete complex grade-level tasks

Despite the 2022 FLS baseline being more comprehensive than ASER (covering 20 languages and multiple cognitive sub-skills), the results confirm ASER's trend: a large proportion of Indian children complete Grade 3 without meeting foundational learning benchmarks.
	 


 
SECTION 7: DIGITAL LITERACY AND FLN (ASER 2024)
	 
7. Digital Access and Literacy (Age 14-16)
ASER 2024 included a digital literacy section for the first time at the national household level, covering children aged 14-16. Digital literacy is increasingly relevant to FLN as technology-based interventions (EdTech, digital TLM, remote teaching) become central to India's FLN strategy.
