7.1 Digital Skills Assessment
Children were tested on three tasks: setting an alarm, browsing for information, and finding and sharing a YouTube video.
 
•       Over 75% of children who were given the tasks completed them successfully
•       Largest gender gap: Setting an alarm (81.5% boys vs 72.4% girls)
•       Notable exception: In Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, and Kerala, girls either outperform boys or are at the same level
•       States with lower smartphone access and digital skills: Bihar, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh
 
7.2 Digital Learning and FLN
The EAC-PM FLN Report highlights both the opportunity and the risk of digital tools in FLN:
 
•       Digital tools can supplement classroom FLN instruction and enable personalized learning
•       India launched Vidyanjali 2.0 — an online platform connecting volunteers with schools for learning recovery
•       Risk: Digital access is unequal; over-reliance on digital tools without addressing the language-medium problem may entrench existing gaps
•       Gender gap in digital ownership (36.2% boys vs 26.9% girls own smartphones) may widen FLN disparities if digital delivery is the primary mode
 


 
SECTION 8: STRUCTURAL CHALLENGES
	 
8. Key Structural Challenges to FLN in India
8.1 Language as the Central Challenge
Both the EAC-PM FLN Report and ASER data consistently highlight language as the most critical — and most underdressed — challenge in FLN delivery in India.
 
•       India has 22 official languages (Eighth Schedule) and over 19,500 mother tongues
•       In the last 50 years, India has lost over 220 languages; 197 are classified as endangered by UNESCO
•       40% of children globally are taught in a language they do not speak at home — India has a particularly acute version of this problem
•       25% of primary school children in India face moderate to severe learning disadvantage due to language mismatch (World Bank, 2021)
•       FLS 2022 found that Tamil, Konkani, Assamese, and Bodo perform most poorly — all languages with high print complexity or regional underinvestment
•       Severe shortage of trained language teachers, especially for minority and tribal languages
 
8.2 Learning Loss from COVID-19
The pandemic had a catastrophic impact on FLN progress in India, with effects still visible in the 2024 data despite significant recovery.
 
•       Around 250 million students were affected by school closures in India
•       92% of Indian students lost at least one specific language ability (UNICEF, 2022)
•       82% lost at least one specific mathematics ability (UNICEF, 2022)
•       Between 2019 and 2022, globally the share of 10-year-olds unable to read simple text rose from 57% to 70% — India's ASER data mirrors this trend
•       ASER 2022 showed sharp learning declines in virtually every state; 2024 shows recovery but not yet to 2018 levels in many states
 
8.3 Teacher Quality and Training
•       Over 80% of schools received FLN-related in-person teacher training — but quality and follow-through vary widely
•       Many teachers lack metalinguistic understanding needed to teach phonological awareness and decoding effectively
•       Multigrade classrooms (two-thirds of Std I-II classrooms) make structured FLN delivery challenging
•       Teacher attendance has improved (85.1% to 87.5%) but still leaves significant instructional time gaps
 
8.4 Assessment Quality and Frequency
The EAC-PM report notes that FLN-focused assessments in India (NAS, FLS) do not yet cover all critical reading sub-skills:
 
•       NAS covers verbal reasoning, literacy knowledge, phonological awareness, decoding — but mapping shows gaps in language structure assessment at lower grades
•       FLS 2022 covers phonological awareness and decoding at Grade 3 — first-of-kind nationally but needs expansion to annual tracking
•       Children's discomfort with formal assessment formats (especially in non-dominant languages) may underestimate true abilities
•       No single system provides annual, grade-level, multi-skill FLN tracking at the scale needed for real-time course correction
 
8.5 Equity Gaps
Equity Dimension
	Key Data Point
	Gender (reading)
	Girls generally at parity or better in reading; gap is small at all grades
	Gender (numeracy)
	Girls score lower than boys in numeracy nationally (FLS 2022)
	Gender (digital)
	Boys outperform girls significantly in digital ownership and skills
	Rural-urban
	ASER surveys only rural areas; urban FLN data is less systematically tracked
	School type
	Private school children consistently outperform government school peers at all grades
	Girls out-of-school
	7.9% of 15-16 yr girls not enrolled; >10% in MP, UP, Rajasthan, Mizoram, Gujarat
	Region
	North-east states (Assam, Nagaland, J&K) and BIMARU states lag in both literacy and numeracy
	 


 
SECTION 9: RECOMMENDATIONS AND WAY FORWARD
	 
9. Recommendations and Way Forward
9.1 Immediate Priority Actions
•       Accelerate implementation of NEP 2020's mother-tongue mandate; develop and distribute FLN materials in all regional and tribal languages, not just the 22 scheduled languages: Mother tongue instruction
•       Scale up in-person FLN teacher training with classroom observation feedback loops; move from one-time training to continuous professional development: Teacher capacity
•       Expand FLS to annual tracking across grades 1-3 in all states; integrate phonological awareness and oral language comprehension into state-level assessments: Assessment reform
•       Dedicate proportionally greater NIPUN funding and technical support to Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan, Assam, Jammu & Kashmir, and the North-east where Std III and Std V outcomes remain critically low: Target laggard states
 
9.2 Medium-Term Strategies
•       Shift focus from enrollment (already improving) to ECCE quality — trained ECCE teachers, play-based curricula, structured language exposure in mother tongue at Anganwadis: Pre-primary quality
•       With 52% of government primary schools being 'small schools', develop dedicated multigrade FLN pedagogy materials and support systems: Small school support
•       Design gender-responsive numeracy programmes from Grade 1; address cultural and socialization factors that produce gender gaps in arithmetic: Gender equity in numeracy
•       Deploy low-bandwidth, offline-capable digital FLN tools that work in mother-tongue medium; close the gender gap in digital access before scaling digital-first FLN delivery: Digital FLN
•       FLN outcomes are tightly linked to child nutrition; strengthen convergence between MDM, PM POSHAN, and FLN programmes — especially in high-poverty districts: Nutrition integration
 
9.3 Governance and Monitoring
•       Track all states annually on the 5-domain FLN Index (Educational Infrastructure, Access, Basic Health, Learning Outcomes, Governance) and publicly release results to drive competitive improvement: State FLN Index
•       Push FLN monitoring down to the district and school level; link teacher and school head performance assessments to FLN outcomes: District-level accountability
•       Mandate participation by private schools in national FLN assessments to generate a complete national picture — currently ASER and FLS underrepresent private school learning gaps: Private school data
•       Conduct a mid-course assessment in 2025 to evaluate state-level readiness to meet NIPUN Bharat's 2026-27 universal FLN target, identifying states that need emergency course-correction: 2026-27 readiness audit

OVERALL ASSESSMENT: India has made measurable progress on FLN inputs and enrollment. However, with fewer than 1 in 4 Std III government school children able to read at grade level, and the NIPUN Bharat 2026-27 universal FLN deadline less than two years away, a significant gap between ambition and achievement persists. The pandemic recovery is real but incomplete. Language-medium instruction, teacher quality, and equity for girls and marginalized communities remain the critical bottlenecks.
	 
 
Sources and Methodology
This report synthesizes data from two primary sources:
 
•       ASER 2024 (Annual Status of Education Report, Rural): Published January 28, 2025 by Pratham. A household survey of 6,49,491 children across 17,997 villages in 605 rural districts. Survey covered children aged 3-16 years with assessments in reading, arithmetic, and (for the first time) digital literacy for age 14-16.
•       EAC-PM State of Foundational Literacy and Numeracy Report (2nd Edition, 2023): Published by the Institute for Competitiveness, mandated by the Economic Advisory Council to the Prime Minister (EAC-PM), supported by USAID and Room to Read. Includes the FLN Index across 36 indicators in 5 domains, FLS 2022 data analysis, and state-level scorecards.
 
All data cited is from original source documents. Figures are reported as published; where exact values were available in tabular format from ASER 2024, they are reproduced as-is. FLS 2022 percentage distributions are sourced from the EAC-PM report's analysis of Ministry of Education/NCERT/UNICEF data.
📝 Technical Note: Why This Project Prioritizes Computer Vision & Coordinate Mapping Over Standard OCR/ICR
The Core Problem: Traditional  OCR (Optical Character Recognition) is engineered strictly for machine-printed text, and standard **ICR** (Intelligent Character Recognition) is limited to recognizing isolated handwritten characters inside structured boxes. 
The Challenge: To keep the assessment highly engaging for young learners, our worksheets preserve original, creative, freehand child actions—such as drawing matching lines, tracing paths, and looping scattered clusters[cite: 187, 224, 226, 229]. Standard OCR and ICR fail completely here because they cannot interpret multi-directional pencil lines, overlapping loops, or spatial drawings.
The Solution: Rather than forcing children into restrictive text boxes, this project uses an Advanced Computer Vision (OpenCV) and Spatial Coordinate Mapping pipeline. The backend detects the exact (X, Y) coordinates of a child's hand-drawn marks and matches those paths directly against our Master Answer Key JSON coordinates. This preserves 100% of the creative formatting while achieving automated grading accuracy.












      








  








Automated Question Paper
Class :- 1
Question set :- class 1
500 Generated Question set :- Class1_Math_Worksheets_Sets001-100


Class :- 2

500 Generated Question set :- Class2_qp_500_set


Class :- 3

FLN Levels Structure
FLN Levels Structure
What this document is:
This workbook is the master reference for the Annam.ai FLN Funsheet Curriculum — a screen-free, paper-based foundational numeracy programme designed for sub-foundational learners in Grades 1–4 in rural India.
Each tab represents one curriculum level. For every level you will find: the core concept being taught, the skill focus, the sub-levels with example funsheet tasks, the AI regression logic (what happens when a child fails), teacher notes for in-class delivery, and AI engine notes for the backend team.




How the AI engine works with this curriculum:
When a child fails a funsheet, the AI does not simply repeat the same task. It diagnoses the specific failure mode — whether it is a perceptual threshold error (quantities look too similar), a cognitive regression (the child cannot handle the current abstraction level), or a confidence issue — and generates the next day's sheet accordingly. Every sheet also includes at least one task the child has previously passed, placed at the start, to reduce test-start anxiety. Creative tracing activities (Levels 12 and 24) are always embedded after hard sequencing tasks as confidence anchors.


Adaptive Progression Framework
Level Structure
Each curriculum level contains:
      * Level X.0 → Mastery Level
      * Level X.1 → Easier Remediation Level
      * Level X.2 → Further Remediation Level
      * Additional remediation levels may be added as required for the concept.
The number of sub-levels is not fixed and depends on the complexity of the skill being taught.


Promotion Logic
Success Path
If a child successfully completes Level X.0:
→ Promote to Level X+1.0
Example:
1.0 Passed → 2.0
2.0 Passed → 3.0


Remediation Path
If a child fails Level X.0:
→ Assign Level X.1
If Level X.1 is failed:
→ Assign Level X.2
If Level X.2 is failed:
→ Continue to deeper remediation levels where available.
The AI progressively reduces difficulty while targeting the same competency.


Recovery Path
If a child passes a remediation level:
The child is returned to the parent mastery level.
Example:
1.0 Failed
↓
1.1 Failed
↓
1.2 Passed
↓
1.0 Reattempt
↓
1.0 Passed
↓
2.0
This ensures mastery before progression.
________________


Teacher Intervention Logic
A child is flagged for teacher intervention when:
      * The child fails the mastery level twice after remediation.
      * The child repeatedly struggles with the same competency.
      * Multiple remediation attempts do not result in mastery.
Dashboard Alert
Status: ⚠ Needs Support
Competency: Quantity Comparison
Current Level: 1.0
Remediation Attempts: 2
Recommendation:
Teacher-guided intervention required.




  
 


Level 1: Quantity  Comparison
Level 1: Quantity Comparison 
Concept
      * Comparing Quantities (Equal, More, Less)
Description
      * In this level child observes two group of objects and determine whether they contain the same quantity, greater or less.
      * No counting is required; only visual observations
      * Learning Outcome
By the end of this level, the child will be able to:
      * Recognize equal quantities
      * Identify groups with more or less objects
      * Compare quantities visually.
      * Develop observation and comparison skills.
      * Build a foundation for future mathematical operations.
Skills Developed
Cognitive Skills
      * Visual discrimination
      * Quantity recognition
      * Logical thinking
      * Comparison skills
Mathematical Skills
      * Understanding equal, more and less
      * Quantity awareness
Foundational Skills
      * Attention to detail
      * Decision making
      * Pattern observation

                                            SECTION B - More/Less

This will contain 2 columns, and the child has to match the equal things.

Level 2: Odd One Out
Level 2: Odd One Out
Concept
Odd One Out (Classification and Differentiation)
Description
      * This level develop the ability to identify objects that do not belong to a particular group.
      *  Children observe similarities and differences between objects and classify them based on common attributes such as shape, size, colour, quantity or category.
The difficulty decreases across sub-levels. The harder activities require children to identify differences in quantity or any other characteristics, while easier activities involve obvious differences between objects.
Objective
Identify the item that does not belong to the group.
Learning Outcome
By the end of this level, the child will be able to:
      * Identify similarities and differences among objects.
      * Classify objects based on common characteristics.
      * Recognize an item that does not belong to a group.
      * Develop logical reasoning and observation skills.
      * Make decisions based on visual information.
Skills Developed
Cognitive Skills
      * Classification
      * Observation
      * Logical reasoning
      * Critical thinking
Visual Skills
      * Visual discrimination
      * Pattern recognition
      * Detail identification
Foundational Skills
      * Decision making
      * Problem solving
      * Categorization


Real-Life Connection
Children use classification skills in everyday situations such as:
      * Finding a toy that does not belong in a set.
      * Identifying a fruit among vegetables.
      * Recognizing a different shape among similar shapes.


These activities help children understand similarities, differences, and organization in their environment.

Level 3: Matching + Tracing Lines
Level 3: Matching + Tracing Lines
SECTION A: MATCHING
Concept
Matching and Association
Description
This level helps children develop matching and association skills by identifying relationships between objects, shapes, quantities, and concepts. 
Through matching activities, children learn to observe similarities, recognize connections, and pair items that belong together. 
These activities strengthen observation, classification, logical reasoning, and problem-solving skills.
Objective
Develop matching and association skills.
Learning Outcome
By the end of this level, the child will be able to:
      * Identify relationships between objects.
      * Match similar items correctly.
      * Associate quantities with corresponding groups.
      * Recognize shapes and their real-world representations.
      * Understand simple opposite concepts.


Skills Developed
Cognitive Skills
      * Association
      * Classification
      * Logical reasoning
      * Problem solving
Visual Skills
      * Visual discrimination
      * Observation
      * Shape recognition
Foundational Skills
      * Attention to detail
      * Memory
      * Decision making
Real-Life Connection
Children use matching skills in everyday situations such as:
      * Matching socks into pairs.
      * Matching objects with their shadows.
      * Pairing similar toys together.
      * Identifying opposite concepts like big-small or tall-short.
      * Matching shapes with objects around them.
These activities help children make meaningful connections between concepts and real-world experiences.


SECTION B: TRACING LINES
Objective
Develop fine motor control.




3.0

SECTION A: MATCHING


Description
Children match words or pictures.
Activities

      * Relation Matching
      * Quantity Matching

      1. Match the opposite:

A. Short

B. Circle

D. Star

Dog → ?
A. Nest
B. Water
C. Puppy 
D. Cat




      4. Mixed
Fish → ?
A. Tree
B. Water 
C. Sky
D. Ball

Level 7: Addition through objects
Level 7: Addition Through Objects & Numbers
Objective
      * Understand addition as combining two groups of objects and finding the total quantity.


Description
      * In this level, students learn the concept of addition by combining groups of objects and counting the total. Activities gradually progress from visual object-based addition to numerical addition. Students first count objects, convert them into numbers, and then solve the addition problem. All addition problems are within 10 and do not require carrying.
Example:
🍎🍎🍎 + 🍎🍎 = ?
___ + ___ = ___


Learning Outcome
      * Students can combine two groups of objects, represent them using numbers, and solve addition problems within 10 without carrying.

Level 8: Subtraction(1-10)
Level 8: Subtraction Through Objects & Numbers
Objective
Understand subtraction as taking away objects from a group and finding the remaining quantity.
Description
In this level, students learn subtraction through visual and object-based activities. Students count the objects, identify the quantity being removed, and determine how many objects remain. Activities gradually connect visual representations to numerical subtraction. All subtraction problems are within 10 and do not require borrowing.
Example:
🍎🍎🍎🍎🍎 − 🍎🍎 = ?
___ − ___ = ___
Learning Outcome
Students can determine the remaining quantity after taking away objects and solve subtraction problems within 10 without borrowing.

Level 9: Pattern Recognition+Draw by Tracing
Level 10: Pattern Recognition + Draw by Trace


Section A: Pattern Recognition
Objective
Recognize, understand, and complete visual and numerical patterns.
Description
This level helps children identify, understand, and extend patterns using shapes, objects, symbols, and numbers. 
Children learn to recognize repeating sequences, count objects within patterns, identify missing elements, and predict what comes next. 
Section A: Pattern Recognition

Learning Outcome
      * Students identify repeating patterns and count correctly.
      * Recognize missing elements in a sequence.
      * Complete visual patterns accurately.
      * Complete number patterns accurately.
      * Predict the next item in a pattern.
Skills Developed
      * Pattern recognition
      * Counting
      * Observation


Section B: Draw by Tracing  
Objective
Improve pencil control and number formation.
Learning Outcome
Students can trace and draw simple figures and shapes.
Skills Developed
      * Fine motor skills
      * Hand-eye coordination
      * Shape recognition
Assessment Criteria
      * Proper tracing
      * Controlled hand movement

      * Number comparison
      * Logical reasoning

10.2


Level 12: Tens and Ones
Level 12: Tens and Ones
Objective
Develop an understanding of place value by identifying tens and ones in two-digit numbers.
Description
      * In this level, students are introduced to the concept of B using visual representations such as bundles, blocks, sticks, beads, and objects. 
      * Children learn that a two-digit number is made up of tens and ones and practice identifying, counting, and representing numbers accordingly. 
      * This level acts as a bridge between single-digit numbers and larger numbers, helping students build a strong foundation for understanding numbers from 11 to 30.
Learning Outcome
Students can identify the tens and ones in a two-digit number and represent numbers using groups of tens and individual ones.


12.0
Main Level: 12.0
Description
      * This level contains standard place-value questions where students identify tens and ones in numbers from 11 to 30. 
      * Activities include counting bundles, matching representations to numbers, and determining the number of tens and ones in a given numeral.
Example Activities
Question 1
14
Tens = ___
Ones = ___
________________


Question 2
🟦 = 10
🟨 = 1
🟦 🟨 🟨 🟨
Number = ___
________________


Question 3
1 Ten + 8 Ones = ___
Assessment Criteria
      * Correctly identifies tens and ones.
      * Matches representations with numerals.
      * Represents numbers using tens and ones.



Level 12.0 – Set 1
      1. 14 → Tens = ___, Ones = ___
      2. 🟦 🟨 🟨 → Number = ___
      3. 1 Ten + 8 Ones = ___
      4. 21 → Tens = ___, Ones = ___
      5. 🟦 🟦 🟨 🟨 🟨 → Number = ___
      6. 1 Ten + 5 Ones = ___
      7. 18 → Tens = ___, Ones = ___
      8. 🟦 🟨 🟨 🟨 🟨 → Number = ___
      9. 2 Tens + 2 Ones = ___
      10. 27 → Tens = ___, Ones = ___

12.1
Sub-Level: 12.1
Description
      * This sub-level contains easier questions with strong visual support. 
      * Students work primarily with pictures, bundles, and grouped objects to understand the relationship between tens and ones. 
      * The focus is on recognizing groups of ten and counting remaining objects.
Example Activities
🟦 = 10
🟦 🟨 🟨
How many objects are there?
Answer: ___
________________


Circle the group that shows 12.
🟦 🟨 🟨
🟦 🟨 🟨 🟨 🟨
Assessment Criteria
      * Recognizes a group of ten.
      * Counts additional ones correctly.
      * Associates visual representations with numbers.

12.2
Sub-Level: 12.2
Description
      * This sub-level provides additional support for students who are still developing an understanding of place value. 
      * Activities focus on building the idea of a group of ten through counting and grouping exercises. 
      * Students work with simple visual examples and small quantities to strengthen foundational understanding.
Example Activities
Count the objects:
⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
How many stars?
Answer: 10
________________


Make one group of ten:
⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ ⭐⭐
How many tens?
________________


How many ones?

Level 14: Counting + Fun Trace
Level 14: Counting and Tracing
SECTION A: Matching (Object ↔ Number)
Objective
Match quantities with numerals.
Learning Outcome
Students connect objects with numbers.
Skills Developed
                        * Counting
                        * Matching


SECTION B: Fun – Draw by Tracing
Objective
Learning through creative activities.
Activities
                        * Trace animals
                        * Trace shapes
                        * Trace objects
                        * Trace letters
                        * Trace paths
Skills Developed
                        * Creativity
                        * Fine motor control

Level 15: Mixed
Level 15: After, Between & Before
Objective
Check the concept of after numbers, before numbers, and between numbers.
Learning Outcome
Students can identify numbers that come before, after, and between given numbers.
Skills Developed
                           * Number sequencing
                           * Logical reasoning
                           * Number recognition

Level 16: Addition (1-30)
Level 16: Addition Through Objects & Numbers 1-30
Objective
Develop the ability to solve addition problems involving numbers up to 30 without carrying.
Description
In this level, students build upon their understanding of addition by solving problems involving numbers up to 30. Activities include object-based addition, pictorial representations, and numerical addition exercises. Students practice combining quantities and finding totals while strengthening their number sense and calculation skills.
Learning Outcome
Students can solve addition problems involving numbers up to 30 without carrying and accurately determine the total quantity.


16.0
Main Level 16.0


                           * The number range is 1-30
                           * 2 digit addition without carry
                           * 1 page will contain questions with only numerals and other page with object
                           * Numeral- vertical

Level 17: Subtraction (1-30)
Level 17: Subtraction Through Objects & Numbers 1-30
Objective
Develop the ability to solve subtraction problems involving numbers up to 30 without borrowing.
Description
In this level, students extend their understanding of subtraction by solving problems involving numbers up to 30. Through object-based and numerical activities, students learn to remove quantities from a group and determine the remaining amount. The focus is on developing confidence and accuracy in subtraction without borrowing.
Learning Outcome
Students can solve subtraction problems involving numbers up to 30 without borrowing and accurately determine the remaining quantity.


17.0
Main Level: 17.0
Mix of:
                           * Object-based subtraction
                           * Number-based subtraction
                           * Missing difference questions
                           * Number range 1-30
                           * Numeral- vertical
Example:
18 − 4 = ___
🍎×15 − 🍎×3  (This does not mean multiplication)
___ − ___ = ___

Sub-Level: 17.2
Description
Provides additional remediation through simple object-based subtraction activities and guided counting exercises.
                           * Number range 1-30
                           * Numeral- vertical  (very easy)
                           * Will contain both - number and object

Level 18: Ordering
Level 18: Ordering
Objective
Arrange numbers in  Ascending and Descending order.


Description
This will contain 2 Sections:
SECTION A: ASCENDING ORDER
SECTION B: DESCENDING ORDER
The child has to write  the numbers in in ascending or descending form as given in the question. 
The child will understand what is to be done by looking at the example provided. 
Learning Outcome
Students understand sequencing.


18.0
 Main-Level: 18.0
                           1. This level will contain questions containing counting till 30.
                           2. The child need to arrange 5 numbers
Example:-

Level 19: Numbers 31-50
Level 19: Numbers 31-50


Objective
Writing, and sequencing of numbers from 31 to 50.


Description
                           * In this level, students are introduced to numbers from 31 to 50. 
                           * Children practice identifying, reading, writing, and sequencing these numbers through engaging activities such as tracing, number writing, missing number exercises, and number arrangement tasks. 


Learning Outcome
Students can identify, read, write, and arrange numbers from 31 to 50 correctly.
19.0
Main Level: 19.0

Level 20: Skip Counting in 2s/3s
Level 20: Skip Counting 2s/3s


Objective
Develop understanding of number patterns through equal jumps on a number line.


Desciption
                           * students learn to identify and continue number patterns through skip counting by 2s and 3s. 
                           * Children observe sequences where numbers increase by a fixed amount and complete the missing numbers in the pattern. 


Learning Outcome
                           * Develop understanding of number patterns through equal jumps on a number line.
                           * Students can identify, continue, and create simple skip-counting patterns by 2s and 3s using number sequences and visual representations.


Example Activities
🐸 Jump by 2
2, 4, 6, __, 10
🐸 Jump by 3
3, 6, 9, __, 15
Fill in the missing numbers:
12, 14, __, 18




Difference between sublevels:


Level
	Difficulty
	Support
	19.0
	Standard
	No Support on i side of the sheet and frog jump/number line on other side
	19.1
	Easier
	Frog jumps,number lines,hints
	19.2
	Remediation
	Very small patterns,heavy visual support
	

20.0
Main-Level 20.0


Description
                           * This is the main level where students identify, continue, and complete skip-counting patterns by 2s and 3s independently. 
                           * Activities include number sequences, missing numbers, and visual jump patterns. 
                           * Students are expected to recognize the pattern and determine the next numbers without additional guidance.
Example Activities
🐸 Jump by 2
2, 4, 6, __, 10
🐸 Jump by 3
3, 6, 9, __, 15
Fill in the missing numbers:
12, 14, __, 18

20.1
Sub-Level 20.1
Description
                           * This sub-level provides additional support through visual representations and guided skip-counting activities. 
                           * Students use number lines, frog jumps, and partially completed patterns to understand how numbers increase by a fixed amount. 
                           * The focus is on building confidence in recognizing and extending patterns.
Example Activities
🐸 Frog Jump by 2
2 → 4 → 6 → 8 → __
Number Line:
2   4   6   8   __
🐸 Frog Jump by 3
3 → 6 → 9 → __

20.2
Sub-Level: 20.2 
Description
                           * This sub-level is designed for students who need further remediation. 
                           * Activities use smaller number ranges and visual supports to help students understand the concept of repeated jumps before attempting more complex patterns.
Example Activities
2, 4, __
3, 6, __
Count and jump on a number line with highlighted steps.
Assessment Criteria
                           * Understands the concept of increasing by a fixed amount
                           * Completes simple skip-counting patterns with support
                           * Shows readiness to progress to higher difficulty levels


Sub-Level 20.2 – Remedial Skip Counting (Small Number Ranges)

Level 21: Comparison (1-50)
Level 21: Comparison


Objective
Compare numbers and identify greater than, less than, and equal quantities.


Description
                           * students compare two numbers and determine whether one number is greater than, less than, or equal to the other. 
                           * Children use their understanding of number values to identify relationships between numbers and apply the symbols >, <, and = correctly. 


Learning Outcome
Students can compare two numbers and determine their relationship.


21.0
Main Level: 21.0
Activities
                           * Compare two numbers using >, <, =
                           * Identify the greater number
                           * Identify the smaller number
                           * Complete missing comparison symbols
                           * Compare numbers with close values (e.g., 38 and 39, 47 and 45)




  

LEVEL 21.0 (NUMBER ONLY)

21.1
Sub-Level: 21.1
The activities use smaller numbers, clearer differences between values, and visual aids where required. 
Compare numbers with large differences (e.g., 5 and 15)
Features:
                           * Visual support
                           * Numbers already arranged

Level 22: Ordering
Level 22: Ordering


Objective
Arrange numbers in ascending and descending order.


Desciption
                           * In this level, students learn to arrange numbers in the correct order by identifying the smallest and largest numbers within a group. 
                           * Children practice organizing numbers in ascending order (smallest to largest) and descending order (largest to smallest) through a variety of sorting and sequencing activities. 
                           * This level strengthens number sense, comparison skills, and understanding of numerical relationships.


Learning Outcome
Students can organize numbers from smallest to largest and largest to smallest.


22.0
Main Level: 22.0
Sections
Section A: Ascending Order
                           * Arrange numbers from smallest to largest.
Section B: Descending Order
                           * Arrange numbers from largest to smallest.
Example
Ascending:
23, 18, 30, 15
Answer: 15, 18, 23, 30
Descending:
23, 18, 30, 15
Answer: 30, 23, 18, 15
Section A: Ascending Order
Arrange the numbers from smallest to largest.

22.1
Sub-Level 22.1


This sub-level contains questions that are easier than the main level. 
The activities use fewer numbers and larger gaps between values, making it easier for children to identify the correct sequence. 
Example
Ascending:
12, 18, 25
Answer: 12, 18, 25
Descending:
12, 38, 15
Answer: 18, 15, 12
Arrange the numbers from smallest to largest.

22.2
Sub-Level 22.2


This sub-level provides further remediation for students who continue to struggle with ordering numbers. 
Activities include visual supports such as number lines, object groups, and clearly distinguishable quantities to help children understand the concept of arranging numbers in order.
Example
Using a number line:
10   11   12   13   14   15
Arrange:
15, 11, 13
Answer: 11, 13, 15
Section A: Ascending Order
Arrange the numbers from smallest to largest.
